He left the garage without hurrying, the way he had arrived, and did not look back at the door the way a less patient hunter might have, unable to resist confirming the effect of his own work. He did not need to look back. The thread told him everything he needed to know about what he had left behind him, a faint, steady pull settling into place exactly where he had intended it to settle, patient and unremarkable enough that Corin would likely mistake it, for now, for nothing more than his own thoughts circling back to a stranger's unsettling questions.

Vual walked on into the evening without any particular urgency, entirely satisfied with a first contact that had gone exactly the way three days of careful watching had told him it would go. He did not need Corin to understand yet what had actually happened between them, or to notice the thread now settled quietly into him, thin as a held breath and just as easy, for now, to overlook. That understanding, when it finally arrived, would arrive on Vual's schedule and no one else's.

He would return within the week, he decided, giving the thread time enough to settle properly before he asked anything more of it. A soul rushed too soon toward its own unraveling tended to panic and pull away, and Vual had no intention of losing this particular thread to impatience after three days of watching and one careful evening of work. Some prizes were worth losing to haste. This one, he had already decided, was not going to be one of them.

Chapter 26

Two Offers

The investigation had taken him longer than any case in recent memory, weeks of patient, unglamorous inquiry built on nothing more than the faint residue still clinging to Penumbra's edge and the stubborn refusal to let the matter drop the way protocol would ordinarily have allowed. Sariel had learned, over the course of it, to work the way he imagined ordinary investigators worked — asking questions in towns that had no idea what he actually was, weighing testimony and coincidence against each other instead of a single clean instrument reading, building the case slowly instead of confirming it in one decisive contact. It had been slow. It had also, eventually, worked. The trail had led him here, to an unremarkable street outside a garage, and to a man named Corin Vasse who appeared to have no idea any of this concerned him at all.

Sariel stopped short of the garage itself, some old, well-earned caution keeping him at the edge of the block rather than closing the distance at once. He had not expected company. A second figure stood near the garage's side entrance, close enough to Corin that the two of them were plainly mid-conversation, and something in the stranger's posture read immediately as wrong to Sariel's trained eye — too relaxed, too pleased with itself, the particular ease of someone who considered the ground already won. Sariel had hunted enough of Hell's own kind across enough centuries to recognize the shape of one even at this distance, dressed in a mortal's clothes and wearing a mortal's easy smile. Whatever this was, he was not the only interested party who had found Corin first.

He held at the corner a moment longer, cataloguing the scene with the same unhurried discipline he brought to any approach — the stranger's relaxed stance, Corin's own posture, wary but showing no obvious fear yet, the ordinary evening traffic of the street continuing around them with no sign anyone else had noticed anything out of place. Weeks of patient, uneventful investigation had not prepared him for finding someone else already standing at the end of it. He had assumed, without quite examining the assumption, that whatever answer waited for him here would be his alone to find. That assumption, he understood now, had been wrong from the start.

He crossed the remaining distance without hurrying, though every step carried the coiled readiness of a man prepared for the conversation to end in violence if it needed to. The stranger turned before Sariel had said a word, unsurprised in a way that struck Sariel as its own kind of confirmation — this was not a man caught off guard by an interruption, but one who had simply been waiting to see who would arrive first. "Let him go, Vual," Sariel said, no greeting attached to the name, no room in his voice for anything but the demand itself.

Vual's smile did not falter, though something in it sharpened by a small, deliberate degree. "I don't have him tied to anything, Sariel," he said, spreading his hands in a gesture that managed to look both innocent and entirely unbothered by the accusation. "We're only talking." Corin's eyes moved between the two of them, confusion plain on his face, an alarm that suggested he was realizing the conversation had become something considerably larger than he had expected. "Then you won't mind if I talk to him too," Sariel said, and watched Vual's easy composure hold, mostly, save for that one small tightening at the corner of his smile.

Corin found his voice before either of them addressed him directly, though it took him a moment to locate it, his eyes moving once more between the spear at Sariel's side and whatever it was Vual carried that Corin could not yet see. "Somebody want to tell me what's actually going on?" he said, the words sounding more fearful than angry, though his delivery suggested he was aiming for the second. Vual answered before Sariel could, his tone unhurried and warm despite the tension now sitting thick between the three of them. "It's simple enough," he said, eyes on Corin rather than Sariel, as though the hunter beside them barely factored into his calculation at all. "You have something in you that both of us want. He wants to decide what to do with it on your behalf. I only want you to decide for yourself, with everything you'd need to actually understand the decision."

Corin's gaze went to Sariel, a look Sariel read as a wordless plea for context and did not answer directly. Vual pressed on, as though he had read an opening in the silence and meant to use it, the same patient precision in his voice that his manner had carried since Sariel first laid eyes on the two of them together. "You should also know," he said, still to Corin, "that a man doesn't walk away from an offer like this without someone eventually asking who else might be worth the trouble instead. Your friend, for instance. Dez, is it? People who spend their lives near something like you tend to attract attention sooner or later, whether they want it or not." The threat arrived soft, almost companionable, exactly the kind of thing that could be denied later as nothing more than an observation. Corin's face went pale in a way that told Sariel the threat appeared to have landed exactly where Vual had meant it to. A length of fine chain flickered into view at Vual's wrist as he spoke, there and gone again almost before Sariel's eye could fully register it, the first plain confirmation of the weapon Sariel had suspected he carried since he first saw the two of them together.

"Don't listen to him," Sariel said, flat and certain, though even as he said it he understood he had nothing of comparable weight to offer in exchange. "I can't tell you what you are. I can't promise you an answer, and I can't promise nothing more will come looking for one either." He held Corin's eyes a moment, unwilling to soften what came next with anything false. "What I can tell you is that this is still yours to decide. Not his. Not mine. Whatever you choose, choose it because it's actually yours."

It was, Sariel understood even as he said it, a poor trade against everything Vual had just laid on the table. Certainty against uncertainty. An answer against the mere permission to keep searching for one. He had built his entire reputation on protocols that told him exactly what to do in exactly this kind of moment, and none of them had ever prepared him to stand here with nothing more persuasive to offer than the truth. He said it anyway, because it was the only thing he had that was actually his to give.

Corin stood between them a long moment, saying nothing, and Sariel watched something move across his face that he did not fully understand — not the simple fear he had expected, but a colder, stranger stillness underneath it, as though some older instinct in the man had surfaced to meet this exact moment without needing to be asked. Vual watched too, seeming as patient as ever, as though he expected the silence to work in his favor. Sariel was no longer certain of anything at all, watching Corin's expression settle into something neither of them had put there.

"I don't know what I am," Corin said finally, and the words came out steadier than Sariel had expected, steadier than Corin himself seemed to expect judging by the small surprise that crossed his own face as he said them. "But I know what it feels like to have somebody decide something's owed to me before I've even agreed to the price. The answer's no." He was not looking at Sariel when he said it, and he was not looking at Vual either, some middle distance holding his attention instead, as though the words were arriving from somewhere further back than his own immediate fear. It did not sound like righteousness to Sariel. That much he could hear clearly, whatever else remained unclear about the rest of it — only a flat, exhausted refusal that seemed cold at its center, as though it came from some old, unnamed injury whose exact shape Corin had never traced, even in himself.

Vual's expression did not break, but it shifted by a fraction, as though he were reconsidering his approach. "That's a hard word to mean tonight," he said, as though testing the refusal for any give it might still have. "It's the only one I've got," Corin answered, and did not take it back. Vual held the moment a beat longer, his silence suggesting some final calculation, and then inclined his head, unwilling, apparently, to force what patience alone had failed to secure tonight.

"Another evening, then," Vual said, already stepping back, the words sounding less defeated than patient, as though none of this had troubled him. "I told you once already, Corin. I'm not in any hurry." He glanced once toward Sariel, something almost companionable in the look despite everything the two of them had just stood on opposite sides of, and then turned and walked off down the street without any further ceremony. He left without another word to either of them beyond that, and Sariel did not watch him go, his attention already back on Corin, on the look in the man's eyes that Sariel could only read as a strange, cold clarity.

Sariel studied him a long moment, weighing his own protocol against everything he had learned across weeks of investigation and one very short, very strange evening. Corin was not a demon wearing a disguise. He was not, by any measure Sariel's training recognized, a threat requiring elimination. He was also not nothing, and Sariel understood, standing there, that no clean category existed for whatever he actually was. The deviation that had started weeks ago at the edge of a cooling battlefield had led him, in the end, here — a decision with no protocol behind it, made on his own authority and no one else's.

There would be no clean report for this either, no closure his liaison could log the way every other closure of his long service had been logged. He accepted that without much difficulty. Something in the weeks of patient, unglamorous work that had brought him here had already begun teaching him to accept it, one small unlogged decision at a time, long before tonight forced the largest one yet.

"This isn't forgiveness," Sariel said, already turning to go, offering nothing further by way of explanation. "It's borrowed time. Use it well." Corin opened his mouth as though some answer might arrive if given long enough, but Sariel did not wait to hear whether it did. He walked back the way he had come, unfailing still by whatever new definition of the word this night had just written for him, and did not look back to see what Corin made of the silence he left behind.

Corin stood alone outside the garage for a long time after both of them had gone, the street settling back into its ordinary evening quiet as though nothing at all had happened on it. What he had just refused, what he had just been spared, why a stranger with a spear had chosen, in the end, to simply walk away instead of doing whatever it was men like that apparently did to people like him — none of it added up to anything he could explain. He only knew that something in him had answered for itself tonight without waiting for his permission, the same way it had that night in the bar with Dez, some part of him older and surer than the rest, and that he still had no name for it and no particular confidence he ever would.

He thought of Dez, briefly, and of how close the evening had come to reaching him too, a threat spoken so lightly it had almost passed for conversation. He would not tell Dez any of this. He was not sure, standing here now, that he could even find the words for it if he tried — two strangers, a spear and a chain, an offer he had somehow refused without ever fully deciding to refuse it. Some things, he was beginning to understand, simply did not survive translation into the kind of story a friend over a beer could actually make sense of.

He went home without calling Dez, without trying to explain any of it to anyone, carrying the whole strange evening quietly the way he had learned, these past weeks, to carry everything he could not explain. Whatever he was, whatever had almost claimed him tonight and whatever had almost let it, none of it felt finished. It felt, instead, exactly like what the hunter had called it on his way out the door — borrowed, temporary, his to use well or waste, and entirely, unnervingly, still his own to decide.

He did not sleep easily that night, replaying the evening again and again without arriving anywhere new. Underneath the exhaustion, though, he found a small, unfamiliar steadiness he had not expected. He had chosen something tonight, even without knowing what it was he had chosen, or what it might eventually cost him to have chosen it. For now, that would have to be enough.

Chapter 27

A Fissure in the Light

Gabriel had called the Council within hours of the scout's return, and he watched the chamber fill now with the particular grimness of people who appeared to know, in outline, what they had gathered to discuss. Belial had beaten them to the tomb. Lament was no longer a rumor in the Codex Mortalis — it was a weapon sitting in an enemy's hand, capable, in theory, of matching Solmire itself. The question in front of them was no longer whether the weapon existed. It was what to do about the fact that it already did.

Uriel spoke first, as Gabriel had known he would, his voice carrying what Gabriel had long since learned to recognize as the particular heat of a man who treated hesitation as its own kind of failure. "Every hour we wait is an hour Belial spends learning to wield it," he said, both hands flat against the Great Table of Covenant as though he might will the room into motion through sheer contact with the stone. "Strike now, before he masters it, and we face a dangerous weapon in unpracticed hands. Wait, and we face it in hands that know exactly what they're doing." Raphael answered from across the table, quieter but sounding no less certain in his own register. "And if we strike before we understand what that weapon actually does, we hand him a battlefield demonstration of its full power at our own expense," he said. "Caution isn't the same as paralysis, Uriel. We don't even know what Lament costs to wield, or what it does to the one who wields it."

Gabriel let the exchange run its familiar course, the same two positions they had argued through since long before this particular crisis, and found his own attention drifting, against his will, toward the chair that remained conspicuously empty at the table's head.

He had convened enough of these gatherings by now to recognize the particular rhythm this one had already fallen into, the same two arguments returning in slightly different clothes over a dozen different crises. It rarely resolved anything cleanly. It did, at least, force every angle of a problem into the open before a decision hardened around it, and Gabriel had learned to value that much even when the argument itself grew tiresome to referee. What troubled him today was not the argument. It was how little of his attention he actually had left to give it.

The scout's report sat closed on the table in front of him, a thin folio he had already read twice before the Council convened. Belial had reached the tomb first. The guardian that had turned Belial away on his own first attempt had, by the second, simply let him pass — some threshold of understanding met, some price paid that the report could only speculate about from a careful distance. Lament was gone from the Weeping Sepulcher and gone, now, from any hand but his. Gabriel had read the details three times over and still felt no closer to understanding what, precisely, that meant for the war they were all still fighting.

Zadkiel's voice cut through the argument before it could sharpen further into something less productive, the same measured, procedural weight Gabriel remembered from the Council that had first authorized the exploration itself. "Before either of you commits this body to a decision," Zadkiel said, "I want it formally noted that we are deliberating a war footing in the continued absence of our field commander." His silver eyes moved once around the table, settling nowhere in particular, judgment radiating from the look itself rather than from any accusation in his tone. "Not as a rebuke. As a fact this Council will need on record, whatever we decide today."

No one answered him directly. Gabriel felt the weight of the observation land where Zadkiel appeared to have intended it to, a formality that was not really a formality at all. He was still searching for some way to address it himself when the chamber doors opened, and the conversation the Council had been having about Miguel's absence became, all at once, a conversation it no longer needed to have.

Miguel crossed the threshold like a man walking through water, each step arriving a fraction slower than it should have, his hand closed hard around Solmire's hilt in a grip that looked less like readiness than dependence. Gabriel had not seen him this gaunt since the earliest days after the sword's forging, hollows carved beneath his eyes that no amount of rest seemed able to fill anymore. His gaze swept the chamber as he approached the table, catching on nothing and no one for more than a moment, as though he were tracking something in the room that the rest of them could not see.

Gabriel rose halfway from his seat, some instinct toward his brother overriding the Council's business entirely for a moment, and then sat back down at Miguel's small, sharp shake of the head. Whatever this was, Miguel seemed intent on enduring it standing, and Gabriel had learned, across too many months now, when to let him.

It had not always been this way between them. Gabriel could still remember, with an ease that felt almost cruel now, a version of Miguel who would have crossed the chamber himself to sit at Gabriel's side before any Council business began, who would have made some low, private joke meant for no one but his brother before turning to face whatever the room demanded of him. That version of Miguel had been retreating by inches for longer than Gabriel liked to admit, each retreat small enough on its own to explain away, until the sum of them had become this — a man standing at the head of his own war council, surrounded by allies, and utterly, visibly alone.

No one else in the chamber moved to greet him either. Uriel gave him the barest nod, his attention already looking half-returned to his own argument. Raphael's eyes lingered a beat longer, as though he were making some private assessment he did not share aloud. Zadkiel alone met Miguel's arrival without any adjustment to his own bearing at all, watching him take his place with the same even, unhurried attention he had given every other detail of the meeting so far.

Uriel resumed his argument as though the interruption had cost him nothing, laying out Belial's likely movements with what Miguel had always taken for the same relentless precision Uriel brought to every strategic assessment, and Miguel tried, for a while, to follow the shape of it. The words kept arriving a half-second delayed, oddly distant even at this range, and beneath them, faint but unmistakable, Miguel heard it again — the low, patient laughter that had chased him through every restless hour since the forge-dream first found him. It had never fully left. It had only been waiting for a silence quiet enough to be heard in.

He fixed his eyes on Uriel and, for one impossible instant, saw something else laid over the archangel's familiar features — a flicker of amusement that did not belong to Uriel at all, a faceless smith's mouth curling at the edges as though the whole Council, the whole desperate strategy session, were some private joke only the smith found genuinely funny. Miguel blinked, and Uriel's face was only Uriel's face again, mid-sentence, showing no sign that anything had happened. His pulse hammered against his own ribs. No one else in the chamber appeared to have seen anything at all.

He gripped Solmire's hilt tighter, willing the sword's familiar hum to drown out whatever this was, the way it had drowned out smaller doubts before. It offered him nothing this time, silent and inert against his palm, no comfort in its usual quiet certainty. He was alone with the laughter and the borrowed smile both, and no amount of gripping the hilt seemed able to change that. He tried, for a moment, to fix his attention on the actual words still moving around the table — casualty projections, a proposed staging ground, the ordinary machinery of war he had spent centuries fluent in — and found the words sliding past him regardless, thin and distant against the much louder thing crowding the inside of his own skull.

Somewhere beneath the panic, a colder thought surfaced, one he wanted gone and couldn't shake. This was the same laughter from the dream that had first found him weeks ago, the same voice that had left him doubting whatever certainty Solmire's forging had once given him. He had told no one about it, had turned Gabriel away at his own door rather than explain it, had almost gone to Raphael and stopped himself out of something that felt, in the moment, indistinguishable from shame. Sitting with it now, in front of the entire Council, he understood exactly how much that silence had cost him. It had cost him this — a private horror with nowhere left to go except straight through the only thing in the room solid enough to break.

"Silence," Miguel said, low at first, almost swallowed by Uriel's ongoing argument. When no one stopped, when the laughter kept threading through every word still being spoken around him, he said it again, louder, and then a third time with his fist already falling. "Silence!"

The blow landed on the Great Table of Covenant with a sound like the chamber itself had cracked open. A spiderweb of fissures raced outward from the point of impact, splitting the pristine marble that had stood unblemished through every Council this hall had ever hosted, and the entire room fell into a silence far more total than the one Miguel had been demanding. There was no command left in him by the time the blow landed, nothing that resembled the voice of a field commander at all — only rage with nowhere left to go, grief that had run out of quieter shapes to wear, spending itself against the one surface in the room solid enough to answer it.

Raphael watched the cracks spread across the table's surface, and felt something settle into place inside him that was worse, in its own way, than what looked like shock rippling through the rest of the Council. He had voiced his own quiet doubt about Solmire's nature once before, back in the earliest days after its forging, when doubt had still felt like an indulgence rather than a warning, and he suspected that none of them, himself included, had truly wanted to sit with the answer long enough to pursue it. He had let the question go unspoken since, filed away under things too inconvenient to raise twice. Watching Miguel's fist come down now, watching the man buckle under a weight none of the rest of them could fully see, Raphael understood that he had not been wrong to ask it. He had only been wrong to stop.

He said nothing. There was no need to remind anyone in this chamber what he had once wondered aloud, and even less use in it now, with the damage already spreading across the table in front of them exactly the way his old doubt had once threatened it might. He only watched Miguel, his own horror tangled through with something colder and more familiar: confirmation of a fear he'd carried since Solmire's forging, arriving years too late to do anyone any good.

He thought, briefly, of every small moment since then where he might have raised the question again and chosen not to — a healer's habit, he supposed, of tending to the wound already in front of him rather than the one still gathering somewhere out of sight. He had told himself, each time, that the moment was not the right one, that Miguel carried enough already without one more voice questioning the gift that had made him what he was. Watching the cracks now spreading across the Great Table of Covenant, Raphael understood there had never been a right moment coming. There had only been this one, arrived without his permission, exactly as late as it had always been going to be.

Miguel did not stay to explain himself. He turned from the fractured table and tore out of the chamber without a backward glance, his stride nothing like the slow, careful entrance he had made only minutes before, leaving behind a Council that appeared too stunned to immediately follow and a wound in the room's oldest piece of furniture that no one moved to address. Gabriel rose fully this time, though he made no move toward the door his brother had just gone through, some older instinct telling him that whatever pursuit Miguel might need right now, it was not the kind any of them in this chamber could offer him.

He crossed instead to the table itself and knelt beside it, tracing one splintered line with two fingers, feeling the rough, broken edge where smooth marble had been only minutes before. The stone had held through every crisis this Council had ever weathered, through arguments louder and angrier than anything raised here today, through decades of disagreement that had never once left a mark on it. It had taken Miguel's single blow to finally split it. The damage felt, to Gabriel, like something far larger than a table — a mark of exactly how deep the fracture in his brother actually ran, forced now into plain view where the whole Council had to reckon with it together. Uriel and Raphael had gone quiet behind him, their argument abandoned along with everything else the meeting had originally intended to resolve. Zadkiel's silence, when Gabriel finally glanced back at him, showed no sign of satisfaction at having been proven right. It only carried the plain, settled weight of a Council that now had two things on the record instead of one — the absence Zadkiel had asked them to note before Miguel arrived, and the ruin they had all just watched him leave behind once he had. Whatever signs of quiet concern the Council had shown until today, Gabriel understood, standing over the broken table, that the time for keeping it quiet had just run out.

Chapter 28

What the Choir Won't Sing

Daith had expected another routine translation when Gabriel's summons reached the archive, another fragment of the Codex Mortalis waiting to be catalogued and filed alongside the thousands that had come before it. What Iofiel actually handed him, once he and Kurel had been shown into her scriptorium, was a single sheet of corrupted hymn-script, its edges scorched, its middle stanzas legible only in patches. "We need it translated in full," Iofiel told them both, her own exhaustion plain in the careful way she set the fragment down rather than simply passing it across. "Whatever it says, Gabriel needs the complete text, not a summary. This one matters more than most."

Daith had already begun assessing the fragment's condition before she finished speaking, the ordinary professional habit of a man who had spent centuries reducing chaos like this into something a court of law could actually use. His eyes moved automatically across the scorched margins, cataloguing the extent of the damage the way he catalogued everything — by degree, by category, by what could reasonably be salvaged and what could not. Whatever this hymn actually contained, the shape of the work in front of him looked, at first glance, entirely ordinary.

Kurel, beside him, made no such immediate move. He only looked at the fragment for a long moment, unhurried, the way a man might look at a stranger across a crowded room before deciding whether to speak to them at all. Daith had worked beside him long enough to recognize the particular stillness that came over Kurel before he touched anything he considered significant, though he had never fully understood what separated a significant fragment from an ordinary one in Kurel's own private accounting. Kurel rarely explained it. He simply knew, or seemed to, and the archive had learned over the centuries to trust that instinct even when it slowed the work considerably.

"You already don't like it," Daith said, more observation than question. Kurel did not answer immediately, his gaze still fixed on the scorched parchment. "I don't know what it is yet," he said finally. "That's different from not liking it. Give me the afternoon before you ask me again."

Daith let the remark go, unwilling to spend the first minutes of a new assignment arguing about a feeling Kurel had not even bothered to justify yet. He had learned, across their years working the same archive, that pressing Kurel before he was ready rarely produced anything worth the friction it cost. Better, in his experience, to let the man reach whatever conclusion he was reaching in his own time, and address it directly once he actually had something concrete to say about it.

They worked side by side once Iofiel left them to it, and within the first hour Daith understood exactly how differently they intended to approach the same page. He transcribed letter by letter, mechanical and precise, cross-referencing each surviving character against known liturgical script the moment it appeared legible, building the translation outward from certainty toward the gaps rather than the reverse. Kurel did nothing of the sort. He read the hymn aloud instead, low and unhurried, several times over, before he ever let his fingers touch the parchment itself, as though the words needed to be heard before they could safely be handled.

"You're wasting time," Daith said eventually, not unkindly, more observation than accusation. "The transcription won't move any faster for being recited first." Kurel did not look up from the page. "It isn't about speed," he said. "A name that resists being spoken doesn't reveal itself to a man in a hurry. I'm not reciting it for my own benefit, Daith. I'm listening for what it does when it's said aloud, and you can't hear that from a transcription table."

Daith let the comment pass without argument, chalking it up to the particular reverence Kurel brought to every fragment that crossed his hands, a habit Daith had learned to tolerate over their years working the same archive even when he privately considered it inefficient. He returned to his own methodical pass at the page, confident the two approaches would converge on the same translation eventually, whatever the difference in how each of them arrived there.

It was not an unfamiliar arrangement between them. They had worked side by side on dozens of fragments over the years, Daith's precision and Kurel's patience settling, more often than not, into a rhythm that produced work neither of them could have managed alone. Daith caught details Kurel's slower method sometimes missed in its deliberate unhurriedness; Kurel caught meaning Daith's mechanical precision sometimes flattened past recognizing. Kurel's holds, in Daith's experience, rarely outlasted a single reading — a pause, a second look, and then the work moved again. Daith saw no particular reason today should behave any differently, whatever small friction had already crept into the first hour of it.

He worked in silence for a while after that, filling in the more legible stretches of the hymn's opening stanzas, cross-referencing each fragment of surviving script against the archive's older liturgical records with the same steady confidence that had carried him through every translation before this one. The work went slowly, as damaged fragments always did, but it went the way work always went — forward, incrementally, toward a completed and defensible record.

The difference stopped being merely stylistic within the hour. Daith reached a clean stretch of legible text near the hymn's middle and moved, without pausing to consult Kurel, to seal the section and mark it complete — standard procedure for any passage that met the archive's threshold for confident translation. Kurel's hand closed over the parchment before Daith's seal touched it. "Not yet," he said, quiet but immovable. "There's a name in there. I want to hear it again before you lock it into the record."

"It's legible, Kurel. That's the entire threshold. I'm not required to wait for your instincts on top of it." Daith kept his voice level, though something in Kurel's refusal had already begun to needle at the edge of his patience. "A name that resists being pronounced isn't something procedure gets to overrule," Kurel said. "You seal it now, and whatever it is stays sealed the way we first understood it — wrongly, if we're wrong. I'd rather be slow than certain and mistaken."

Daith withdrew his hand from the parchment, though not the argument itself. "Show me, then. If there's something here that actually merits holding the seal, show me the passage instead of asking me to take it on faith." Kurel obliged, turning the fragment so the disputed section faced them both, tracing the relevant line with one careful finger. To Daith's eye it looked no different from the dozen surrounding lines he had already sealed without incident, the same careful liturgical hand, the same familiar cadence of praise and invocation that ran through most of the Codex's older hymns. He said as much.

"Read it aloud," Kurel said. "Not scanned. Spoken." Daith obliged more out of exasperation than genuine curiosity, reading the line at his usual brisk pace, and heard nothing in his own voice worth remarking on. "There. Nothing. Whatever you're hearing, Kurel, I'm not hearing it too." Kurel's expression did not change, patient in a way that read, to Daith, as its own quiet form of stubbornness. "Then read it again. Slower this time. I'm not asking you to believe me. I'm asking you to actually listen before you decide there's nothing to hear."

Daith read it again, if only to close the argument rather than out of any real expectation the second reading would differ from the first. This time, faint but unmistakable, he heard his own voice catch on the final syllable of the line's last word, some small, involuntary hitch he could not immediately account for. He read it a third time to be certain, and the hitch repeated itself, precisely where it had the second time, though the exact sound of the word itself seemed, absurdly, to shift slightly between attempts. He set the parchment down without comment, some of his earlier certainty visibly shaken, though he was not yet willing to say so aloud.

Kurel watched Daith set the parchment down and read, in the sudden care of the gesture, that whatever certainty had carried his colleague through the last hour had just taken a real blow. He had heard the same shift happen on his very first pass through this stanza, hours ago, though he had said nothing of it then, unwilling to raise an alarm before he understood what exactly he was raising it about. Watching Daith's composure falter against evidence Daith himself had just produced seemed, to Kurel, confirmation enough that his own instinct had not been misplaced.

He had learned, over centuries of handling fragments like this one, to trust a quiet certainty like this before he trusted any transcription built on top of it. Names carried weight in a way ordinary language did not, and a name that resisted a clean, repeatable pronunciation was rarely resisting by accident. He did not yet know what this particular resistance meant. He knew only that Daith's own instincts, usually so reliable in matters of procedure, had nothing built into them for a fragment that behaved this way, and that gap seemed, to Kurel, worth taking seriously in its own right.

"The past does not forget," Daith said, recovering his composure quickly, an edge of stubbornness in his voice that Kurel had learned, over their years working together, not to mistake for actual certainty. "Neither do I. We transcribe what's here, entire, or we don't transcribe it at all." Kurel did not release the parchment. "What's here includes a word that corrupts itself every time someone tries to say it out loud. Are you transcribing that 'entire' too, Daith, or pretending you didn't just hear it happen?"

Daith had no immediate answer for that. Kurel pressed on, quieter now, some of the earlier heat gone out of his own voice. "Each name is a key, Daith. Each key, a universe. I'm not refusing to transcribe out of stubbornness. I'm refusing because I don't know what door we're about to open, and neither do you."

Daith's jaw tightened, some private calculation visibly working itself out behind his eyes before he spoke again. Kurel had seen that particular stillness in him before, usually in the moments just ahead of Daith conceding a point he did not want to concede. "You think I don't understand what's at stake in getting this wrong," he said finally, an edge of something almost defensive in it. "I understand it exactly as well as you do, Kurel. I've spent longer in this archive than you have." Kurel let his own expression soften, fractionally, at that. "I never said you didn't understand it," he said. "I said we disagree about what getting it wrong actually looks like. You're afraid of an incomplete record. I'm afraid of a complete one we don't actually understand yet. Those aren't the same fear, even if they both sound like caution from the outside." He watched the distinction land, or seem to, some of the tension easing out of Daith's shoulders even if his expression gave up nothing further.

Daith held his silence a moment longer before answering, ground given without the argument itself being conceded. "Then tell me what we do with a hymn that doesn't even want to be read."

Neither of them had an answer ready. Kurel let the question sit between them on the table alongside the fragment itself, spoken aloud now instead of merely implied, and read in Daith's continued silence something that looked, for once, like the same uncertainty Kurel himself had been carrying since his first pass through the stanza. It was, in its own small way, a rare thing to see in a man who rarely allowed himself to appear uncertain about anything at all. Whatever the hymn actually contained, it had already proven it would not yield to procedure alone, and Kurel suspected neither of their approaches, alone, was going to resolve this tonight.

The scriptorium had gone quiet around them at some point during the argument, the ordinary evening business of Iofiel's other assistants long since finished for the day, leaving the two of them alone with a hymn neither one seemed willing to simply abandon. Kurel set the fragment down between them, careful, as though the parchment itself might still be listening. "We have time before Gabriel needs this," he said. "Not much. But some. Enough to be careful instead of merely fast." Daith looked at the scorched edges a long moment before answering, some of his earlier impatience visibly spent. "Then we use it," he said, "however little of it there is." It was not agreement, not exactly. It was the closest thing to it Kurel had gotten from him all evening, and for now, with the light in the scriptorium beginning to fade and the question of what to actually do with an unwilling hymn still entirely unresolved, he was willing to take it. Whatever came next, they would at least be arriving at it together, however far apart their reasons for getting there still ran.

Chapter 29

The Discrepancy

Daith returned to the scriptorium the next morning no closer to an answer than he had left it, and found, within the first hour, that neither had the argument itself gone anywhere overnight. He and Kurel worked the fragment in an uneasy parallel rather than any real partnership now, each of them circling the same disputed stanza from opposite directions without managing to actually close the distance between their positions. The rest of the hymn had come along steadily enough in the time since, stanza after stanza yielding to Daith's methodical transcription with no further complications. Only the final section remained, the one containing the word that refused to hold its shape, sitting between them like a wound neither of them had found a way to close.

He had spent part of the previous night turning the disagreement over in his own head, testing Kurel's reasoning the way he might test an unfamiliar witness statement against known facts, searching for the flaw that would let him dismiss it cleanly and return to the work procedure demanded. He found, instead, something closer to confirmation than refutation: a search through the archive's own precedent records that night had turned up no comparable case, no other fragment anywhere in the Codex Mortalis whose language had ever behaved this way on the page. The absence unsettled him more than Kurel's caution had. Iofiel expected a complete translation. Gabriel needed it soon. Neither fact had changed overnight, and neither of them, Daith told himself, actually required his own private doubts to be resolved first.

The scriptorium, when he arrived, showed no sign that Kurel had slept any better than he had. The hymn fragment sat exactly where they had left it the night before, undisturbed, the disputed stanza still bearing the faint discoloration where both their hands had rested against it during the worst of yesterday's argument. Kurel was already at the table, reading the final lines under his breath again, the same slow, deliberate cadence Daith had watched him use a dozen times over the past two days without once managing to hear in it whatever Kurel himself was listening for.

He reached for the final stanza mid-morning, telling himself it was simply the next reasonable step in an assignment already running behind schedule, and began transcribing it letter by letter the way he had transcribed everything else in the hymn — without waiting for Kurel's approval this time, without so much as announcing that he had decided to proceed. It was, in the narrowest technical sense, well within his authority to do. The stanza was legible. His methods had never required a second signature before committing anything to the record.

He had not announced his intention beforehand, had barely allowed himself to consider whether he should. Reaching for the stanza anyway was its own argument — asking would have meant risking another refusal, and proceeding without asking meant he did not have to hear one. He told himself this was simply efficiency, the ordinary momentum of a man returning to work he had already half-completed. He did not entirely believe himself, and pressed forward anyway.

Kurel's hand came down over the parchment before Daith's pen reached the disputed word itself, firm enough to stop the transcription outright without quite becoming violent about it. The suddenness of it startled Daith more than he cared to admit, his pen skidding a fraction off its intended line before he managed to still his own hand. "Move your hand, Kurel," Daith said, an edge of genuine anger entering his voice for the first time since the argument had begun the day before. "Not until you tell me why you're in such a hurry to finish something we haven't even understood yet," Kurel answered, not moving the hand at all.

"I'm not in a hurry, Kurel. I'm doing my job." Daith kept his own hand steady around the pen, unwilling to give the moment any more physical drama than Kurel had already introduced to it. "You started transcribing the one line we both agreed needed more attention, without a word to me first, the moment my back was turned long enough for you to try it," Kurel said, his voice carrying none of Daith's rising heat, which somehow made the accusation land harder rather than softer. "That's not doing your job. That's doing an end run around a disagreement you didn't want to keep having."

Daith opened his mouth to object and found, to his own irritation, that he had no immediate rebuttal ready. It had, in fact, been exactly that. He had known, reaching for the stanza, precisely what Kurel's reaction would likely be if he asked first, and had chosen the version of events where he did not have to find out for certain. He said none of that aloud, unwilling to let Kurel know how precisely the accusation had landed.

"We didn't agree it needed more attention, Kurel. You decided it did, and I've been living with that decision for two days now." Daith heard how thin the distinction sounded even as he made it, and pressed on anyway rather than concede the point outright. "There's a difference between respecting your instincts and being held hostage by them indefinitely. I've given you the benefit of the doubt since yesterday morning. At some point that benefit runs out."

"Because an incomplete record helps no one," Daith said, the words arriving with more heat than he had intended, months of professional conviction compressed into a single well-worn argument. "You want to wait until you fully understand something before committing it to paper. I understand that instinct, Kurel, I do. But we don't have the luxury of understanding everything before we act. Sometimes the record has to exist before the understanding catches up to it." Kurel did not remove his hand. "And sometimes," he said, "the record existing before the understanding catches up is exactly how something gets loose that should have stayed contained a little longer. You're not wrong that waiting has a cost. I'm not wrong that moving too fast has one too. We just disagree about which cost is worse."

"Then tell me what you actually think is in that word," Daith said, quieter now, some of the heat draining out of the demand even as he made it. Kurel held his eyes for a long moment before answering. "I don't know," he said. "That's the entire problem, Daith. I don't know what it is, and neither do you, and the not-knowing is exactly the reason I won't let you seal it shut before either of us actually does."

"That's not an answer. That's a reason to keep not answering forever." Daith heard the frustration sharpen his own voice again and made no particular effort to soften it this time. "At some point, Kurel, 'we don't know yet' has to become 'we tried to find out.' You can't hold a hymn hostage to your own instincts indefinitely. Eventually someone has to decide the waiting itself has become the greater risk." Kurel's expression did not change, though something in his posture seemed to settle more firmly, as though bracing against an argument he had heard some version of before and had yet to find persuasive. "Maybe," he said. "But I'd rather be the one who waited too long than the one who spoke a name before anyone understood what answering it would cost. You're right that waiting isn't free, Daith. I've never claimed it was. I just think the other cost is worse, and I think you know that too, somewhere underneath how certain you're trying to sound right now."

The accusation landed closer to true than Daith wanted to concede aloud, and he said nothing for a moment, the silence itself doing more work than any further argument might have. Kurel did not press the advantage. He only waited, patient in the particular way that had always unsettled Daith more than any direct confrontation could have, as though he had all the time in the world and fully expected Daith to eventually arrive at the same conclusion on his own.

Daith withdrew his hand from the parchment eventually, more out of exhaustion than any real concession, and set aside the few opening lines he had already managed to copy before Kurel's hand had stopped him, unwilling to file a transcription he could not finish properly. He spent the remainder of the afternoon reviewing the hymn's less contentious stretches instead, cross-checking his own earlier transcription against the original script for anything he might have rushed, letting the final stanza sit unfinished between them. Kurel said nothing further about the exchange, returning to his own slow, patient reading of the earlier stanzas as though the confrontation had never happened at all, though the covered corner of the parchment stayed exactly where his hand had left it, a small, deliberate boundary neither of them chose to test again that day. He told himself, working back through the safer passages, that he had simply chosen the more efficient use of his time rather than backed down from anything. The explanation didn't sit right, and he knew it — but he kept that particular doubt to himself, unwilling to hand Kurel a victory he had not technically earned.

His pen moved through corrections and annotations across the safer passages with its usual precision, but some part of his attention kept drifting back toward the covered stanza, toward the particular stillness with which Kurel had guarded it all day without once needing to repeat his refusal aloud. Daith had worked beside plenty of colleagues over the centuries who held stubborn opinions about matters of procedure, and he had rarely found himself troubled by any of them the way this particular disagreement was beginning to trouble him now.

Alone that evening, walking the quiet corridor back toward his own quarters, he allowed himself to consider, briefly and privately, whether Kurel might actually be right. It was an uncomfortable thought to sit with, precisely because dismissing it would have been so much easier than examining it honestly. Daith had built his entire career on the principle that thoroughness protected people — that an incomplete record was a record that eventually failed someone who needed it whole. He had not, until now, had real cause to consider that the opposite might also be true — that a record completed too soon could fail someone just as badly.

He thought, walking, of every case in the archive's long history where an incomplete translation had cost someone dearly — a covenant left ambiguous, a testimony filed with gaps a clever advocate later exploited, a judgment overturned because the record simply hadn't held enough of the truth to stand on. He had cited those cases to Kurel more than once over the years, the Perren testimony above all, a case he could still recite from memory, always as settled proof of his own methods. He found himself, tonight, unable to summon a single case in the other direction — no record he could name that had been finished too soon and done comparable damage — and was not certain whether that was because no such case existed, or because he had simply never gone looking for one.

He did not reach any conclusion before he reached his own door. He only knew, letting himself inside, that the certainty he had walked into the scriptorium with two days ago had not survived the argument intact, whatever he had actually said to Kurel's face about it.

Neither of them raised the subject again before the day ended. Kurel would not yield the seal; Daith would not concede the principle; the final stanza sat exactly where it had that morning, unfinished, its corrupted word still refusing to hold a single shape between readings.

He returned to the scriptorium the next morning to find nothing had shifted overnight — except, he noticed, that Kurel had taken to reading the stanza to himself more often than the day before, under his breath, at odd moments between other tasks, as if checking something that would not stay checked. Daith said nothing about it. Something would eventually have to give — Gabriel's patience, if nothing else, or the deadline Iofiel had made clear was not indefinite. For now, the two of them worked around the wound in the hymn rather than through it, each waiting, Daith suspected, for the other to break first.

Chapter 30

What Remains Unnamed

Kurel found Daith in the scriptorium early, before either of them had said a word about the argument that had carried into a second full day without resolution, and set a single folded sheet down on the table between them. "I've drafted a version we can both actually submit," he said. Daith read it through twice before looking up, and found, to his own surprise, that Kurel had transcribed everything — every stanza, every line, the full weight of the hymn rendered exactly as precisely as Daith himself would have demanded, right up until the final stanza. There, in place of the corrupted word and everything around it, Kurel had noted, in a neat editorial hand, a single word of his own: illegible. Their own working transcription, the one they had argued over for two days, still sat untouched in its usual place — this was only the fair copy meant for Gabriel's eyes.

"It isn't illegible," Daith said, more observation than accusation at this point, some of the fight gone out of him after two days of the same argument circling itself without resolution. "'Illegible' isn't entirely a lie," Kurel said, meeting his eyes without flinching from the half-truth of it. "Legible to whom is the part we're leaving unspecified." Daith turned the folded sheet over once more, weighing the compromise against everything he still believed about complete records and the people who eventually needed them. He did not approve of it, not fully. Searching for a real objection sharp enough to voice, he found instead only the shape of one — a single word standing in for a line and a half of script neither of them had managed to defend in two days of trying — and underneath the objection he could not quite finish, something closer to relief than he had expected exhaustion to leave behind. He was not ready to call that erosion agreement. He was willing, at least, to call it honest enough to accept a compromise he might have rejected outright on the first day.

"Gabriel will ask why," Daith said finally, setting the sheet back down between them. "He will," Kurel agreed. "And I'll tell him the fragment's condition made full recovery impossible. Which is true, as far as it goes." Daith studied him a moment, weighing the carefully bounded honesty of that phrasing against everything he had spent two days insisting a record owed the people who relied on it. "As far as it goes," he repeated, not quite able to keep a note of dry skepticism out of his own voice. "That's doing a great deal of work for four words, Kurel." Kurel's expression did not change. "It's doing exactly as much work as it needs to," he said. "No more."

The corrupted line itself, before Kurel struck it from their own record, had carried a single fragment that neither of them had managed to translate cleanly in two full days of trying — not a name, nothing so definite as that, only the barest indication of something, some unspecified presence whose referent the grammar did not identify. Every rendering Daith tried against it collapsed the moment he tried to pin the reference down to anyone or anything in particular. Daith had read the fragment a dozen times across the argument, and each time, saying it aloud, felt a faint pressure gather somewhere behind his own sternum — fainter the first few times, sharper by the last, though the syllables themselves refused, every time, to settle into anything he could actually transcribe — gone again the moment he stopped speaking. He had no ready explanation for the sensation and had not mentioned it to Kurel. Kurel had read the line more times than that, and had said, at one point during the worst of yesterday's disagreement, that some lines simply could not be understood yet.

Daith had dismissed the comment then as more of Kurel's particular reverence talking. He found himself less certain that Kurel's reading of the situation had been wrong, looking at the redacted stanza and the careful, deliberate blank space where an explanation should have gone.

He tried, briefly, to imagine what kind of answer might have satisfied him two days ago, before any of this had started — a clean translation, a defensible record, nothing left ambiguous for some future reader to mistake for a settled conclusion. He could still imagine wanting that. He found he could no longer imagine it being the right thing to want, not for this particular fragment, not after three separate attempts had failed to produce anything either of them could honestly call a translation. Daith began to suspect, watching the blank space where it had been, that Kurel's instinct had never actually been about protecting the hymn from scrutiny at all. It looked, from where Daith now stood, more like an attempt to avoid handing some future reader an answer neither of them could honestly justify yet.

That thought unsettled him more than the argument itself had. Daith had spent his entire career believing that thoroughness was, in every case, the safer choice — that an unresolved gap in the record could create problems for later readers. He was not prepared to abandon that belief wholesale. He was prepared, for the first time in two days, to admit it might not hold universally, and that admission cost him more than any argument Kurel had made aloud.

Their own working transcription still needed attending to before either of them could call this settled, and Kurel did that marking himself rather than letting Daith log it as a simple correction. He drew the archive's own copy toward himself, taking up his pen with a care that looked, to Daith's eye, almost ceremonial — not the brisk efficiency either of them brought to ordinary corrections, but something slower, more deliberate, as though the act of striking the passage from their own record required its own kind of respect. He drew a single line through the corrupted words, once, without hesitation once the pen actually touched the page, and set the sheet aside to dry with the same careful attention he had given every reading of it before this one.

"You could have let me mark it," Daith said, watching him. "I know," Kurel said. "I wanted it to be mine." He offered nothing further by way of explanation, and Daith, for once, did not press him for one.

Daith watched him set the pen down afterward and study the completed page a moment longer than the correction alone required, his expression unreadable, as though some private accounting were taking place behind it that Daith had no way to follow from the outside. It occurred to him, watching, that this was probably the closest either of them would ever come to a kind of small ceremony for a word neither of them had managed to understand — not a funeral, not quite, but something adjacent to one, marked by a man who Daith now suspected took the responsibility of it more seriously than he had initially given him credit for.

"Does it bother you," Daith asked, "leaving it like this? Unfinished?" Kurel considered the question longer than Daith expected before answering. "Less than finishing it wrong would," he said. "I've seen names forced into meanings they didn't actually carry, just because someone needed an answer before the available evidence could support one. I'd rather carry the discomfort of not knowing than hand someone a certainty I manufactured myself." Daith had no immediate response to that, and did not attempt to manufacture one.

Daith carried the finished translation to Iofiel's chambers himself, Kurel beside him, and watched her read it through in the particular unhurried way she read anything that mattered enough to warrant her full attention. Gabriel stood near the window, arms crossed, waiting for her assessment rather than reaching for the pages himself. Daith had rehearsed, on the walk over, several versions of how he might answer if Iofiel pressed the question directly, none of which had felt entirely comfortable to imagine saying aloud. He had settled, in the end, on saying as little as the moment allowed and letting Kurel carry whatever weight the answer actually required, since the redaction had been Kurel's choice to make in the first place.

"The final stanza," Iofiel said eventually, tapping the redacted line. "You're certain this is genuinely illegible, and not simply difficult?" Daith felt Kurel go very still beside him, though neither of them answered immediately.

"The fragment's condition made full recovery impossible," Kurel said finally, choosing each word with visible care. It was not, strictly, false. Daith said nothing to contradict it, and said nothing to confirm it either, letting the silence do whatever work it needed to do. Gabriel studied them both a moment longer than the answer alone seemed to warrant, as though weighing some private calculation of his own, before he finally spoke. "Then we work with what we have," he said. "If something more surfaces later, bring it to me directly." He did not press further, and Iofiel, after a moment's visible hesitation, let the matter rest as well, setting the translation aside among the day's other reports.

Daith watched her hesitation a moment longer than the exchange itself required, wondering whether she suspected more than she had chosen to say aloud. Iofiel had spent longer with the Codex Mortalis than either of them, and it seemed possible to Daith, watching her set the report down with more care than the gesture strictly needed, that she understood exactly how much a redaction like this one could actually be hiding. If she did, she gave no further sign of it, and neither Gabriel nor anything in his own bearing suggested he intended to revisit the question either, at least not today.

"That's it, then," Kurel said quietly, once they had left the chamber and started back down the corridor. "For now," Daith said. "Gabriel isn't the kind of man who forgets a loose thread entirely. He's the kind who files it away until it becomes useful, or urgent, whichever comes first." Kurel considered that a moment. "Then we've bought time, not resolution," he said. "I can live with that," Daith answered. "I wasn't sure I'd be able to say that two days ago."

They walked back to the scriptorium together in a silence that felt, for the first time in two days, less like a standoff and more like something almost companionable. "You still think I was wrong to hold the seal," Kurel said eventually, not quite a question. "I think you were reckless with something we're supposed to be accountable for," Daith said. "I also think, if I'm being honest, that I might have made the same call in your position. I don't love admitting that." The corner of Kurel's mouth lifted, something close to a smile. "I don't need you to love it," he said. "I just needed you to actually consider it, eventually. You did."

Neither of them fully yielded the ground they had defended over the past two days, and neither, walking back into the scriptorium's familiar quiet, particularly needed to anymore. Daith thought he understood now, in a way he had not quite managed before today, why Kurel had been protecting the record from an unjustified conclusion and why he had been willing to fight so hard over a single word. Kurel, for his part, seemed to understand just as clearly why Daith's own instincts had never been simple stubbornness either, whatever heat their disagreement had generated along the way.

They settled back into their usual places at the table without further comment, the earlier tension between them replaced by something quieter and considerably less combative than anything Daith had expected to feel by the end of this particular assignment. He picked up his own pen to begin logging the completed translation into the archive's permanent record, and found himself, for the first time in two days, entirely unbothered by what the record did and did not contain. Some things, he was beginning to accept, simply were not his to finish alone.

The struck-through working transcription went into the archive's deepest records that evening, filed alongside thousands of other fragments the centuries had left imperfect, its final stanza a single ruled line over words for which neither of them had managed to establish a defensible reading, one small unresolved entry among countless others the archive had preserved without assigning them unsupported interpretations. Daith doubted anyone else in the archive would think twice about a struck line on a damaged text; such things were common enough that, as far as he could tell, no one but Daith and Kurel would ever know exactly how much care that particular line had actually required.

It would remain there, Daith supposed, until further evidence made a defensible reading possible — not lost, not destroyed, only unresolved. Someday, perhaps, someone might possess enough evidence and understanding to finish what the two of them had chosen, deliberately, to leave undone. Until then, no conclusion would be entered in the archive.

Daith closed the record drawer himself that evening, the small metal click of it settling into place feeling, for reasons he did not examine too closely, more final than the sound alone should have carried. Whoever eventually opened this particular drawer again, he thought, it would not be him. That would have to be someone else's discovery to make.

Chapter 31

The Island That Appears on No Map

Cassiel found the wounded soul exactly where Silens told him it would be, curled at the base of a collapsed watchtower on a battlefield three days cold, its light guttering low but not yet gone. He knelt beside it the way the Order had taught him to kneel beside any soul Silens led him to — not from technique alone, but from something closer to a vow he had made so long ago he no longer thought of it as a vow, only as habit worn smooth by centuries of grief. This one had been easy enough to find and far harder to steady, a soldier's soul so thoroughly undone by its own death that it took the better part of an hour of quiet, wordless attention before it settled enough to be guided onward. He did not begrudge the hour. It was simply what the work asked.

This was the work, and had been the work for longer than he could easily number — not glory, not conquest, only the slow business of finding what pain left behind and walking it gently toward wherever it needed to go next. Orifiel had told him once, early in his service, that Silens rewarded a particular kind of stillness most soldiers found tedious to hold: patience without a horizon, a willingness to sit with suffering rather than hurry it along toward the next assignment. Cassiel had taken the instruction less as a technique to master than as something closer to a rule of life, and if it had never occurred to him to resent the plainness of it, that was simple gratitude for work worth doing quietly.

He rose from the watchtower's ruins once the soul had finally settled, brushing ash and old dust from his knees, and took a moment to survey the quiet field around him before moving on — not from procedure, but because a place that had recently held violence seemed to him to deserve at least that much witness before he left it. Three days cold, and still the ground carried traces of what had happened here: scorched grass, a banner half-buried in churned earth, the particular stillness of a place not yet settled back into the ordinary rhythm of the world. He had walked a hundred fields like this one across the centuries, and he gave each the same attention, the way one keeps a promise long after anyone else has forgotten it was made.

Silens hummed quietly against his palm as he walked, its steady pulse cataloguing the plane's remaining traces without urgency, doing precisely what it had always done in his hands: finding what needed finding, at whatever pace the finding actually required. He expected, on the last stretch back toward the watch post, an unremarkable end to an unremarkable assignment. Most days ended exactly that way, and he had made a kind of peace with the sameness of it that he suspected some of his fellow Captains would have called small ambition, if they had ever bothered to ask him what he wanted from the years.

He felt the anomaly on his way back from the watchtower, faint enough that a less attentive scout might have mistaken it for residue from the soul he had just guided onward. Silens pulsed differently for this, in a register Cassiel could not place against anything he had catalogued before — not grief, not fear, not the raw disorientation of a fresh death, but something closer to laughter caught and pressed flat in the fabric of the place, old and strange and entirely unfamiliar.

He stood with the sensation a long while, turning it over the way he turned over a difficult confession, patient with his own uncertainty and unwilling to force a conclusion before he was ready to trust it. Silens had never misled him toward nothing before. He did not think it wise to assume, on so little evidence, that it had begun doing so now, though he made himself hold the possibility open rather than dismiss it outright. That, too, was part of what the Order had taught him: certainty arrived at too quickly was its own kind of carelessness.

He tried, standing there, to place the reading against every unusual signature his long service had shown him, searching for some precedent that would let him file it away and continue his rounds. He found none. Wounded souls carried grief, or fear, or the raw disorientation of a death still settling into memory — sensations he knew how to hold. This carried none of that. It carried instead a settled quality he could only compare, imperfectly, to something remembered rather than something felt fresh — an echo of an echo, rather than a wound still open.

He walked a slow circle around the place where the reading had first reached him, testing whether it strengthened or faded with distance, and found that it did neither — only persisted, faint and unchanging, no matter which way he moved. That unsettled him more than any single strange detail could have. A wounded soul had a location, a center a scout could kneel beside. This reading seemed to belong to no single point at all, as though it were less a thing resting on the island than a physical echo diffused through the island itself.

He found Ophaniel at the watch post an hour later, filing his own routine report on the battlefield's remaining stragglers, and mentioned the anomaly in passing rather than as anything requiring escalation. "Anything I should send reinforcements for?" Ophaniel asked, glancing up from her ledger with what looked to Cassiel like mild, procedural curiosity. "Not yet... It might be nothing. I'd rather not waste anyone's time chasing a hunch," Cassiel said. Ophaniel appeared to accept the answer without further comment, as others at the watch often did when Cassiel offered his own assessment, and returned to her ledger.

Cassiel considered, walking away, whether he had been entirely honest in that answer, and found he could not quite decide. It was not nothing — it was, at minimum, the strangest reading Silens had ever handed him. He had decided, somewhere in the moment of speaking to Ophaniel, that he would rather carry the uncertainty alone a while longer than hand someone else a burden he could not yet describe clearly enough to justify. He told himself this was simply good order — verify before you ask others to act — and did not examine too closely whether that was the whole of his reasoning.

He gathered what the assignment might reasonably require before setting out: provisions for several days, nothing that would draw notice if anyone saw him leaving, a decision made as quietly as the reading itself had first reached him. His record had earned him the latitude to take a solo scouting trip without needing to justify it, and he did not expect this one to test that latitude any further than the last. In every practical sense it was an assignment he had taken a hundred times before. Only the reading itself, faint and unlocatable and unlike anything Silens had ever shown him, made the walk out feel different beneath his feet.

The trail took the better part of a day to follow, strengthening only marginally as he moved, offering him nothing so simple as a straight line. He tracked it the way he tended any soul too disoriented to be led directly — small corrections, offered continuously, trusting the accumulated pattern of many uncertain readings over any single confident one. By the time the coastline came into view, unlisted on any chart he carried and unfamiliar in a way that felt like more than an oversight of the cartographers, he had long since stopped expecting the trail to lead him anywhere ordinary.

The trail led him, over the following day, to an island absent from every chart he consulted, its coastline shifting subtly enough between his approach and his landing that he began to doubt the maps were entirely at fault for missing it. Turein's interior unsettled him the moment he crossed the treeline — vegetation growing in slow, deliberate spirals rather than any pattern he recognized, the wind folding back on its own passage in ways that left the light moving strangely across the canopy overhead.

He felt, standing in the heart of it, some faint and unaccountable conviction that this place held something connected to Solmire — not a certainty he could trace to any single sensory detail, only a low recognition he could not yet name. He had felt nothing like it in all his years of scouting. He chose to trust it anyway, the way he had learned, slowly and through long practice, to trust every faint signal Silens had ever handed him, certain or not — not because the feeling demanded trust, but because refusing it, he suspected, would have been its own kind of arrogance.

He moved deeper with the same careful attention he brought to any unfamiliar ground, cataloguing details out of long habit: the spiraling growth repeating at every scale he examined, from the smallest curling fern to the largest tree's own twisting trunk, as though whatever governed this island's growth repeated itself equally at every size. He had scouted strange terrain before. He had never scouted anywhere that repeated a single pattern with quite this much consistency, and the consistency itself, more than any single strange detail, was what persuaded him this place was not simply unusual by accident but shaped by some pattern he did not yet understand.

The light overhead moved oddly too, shifting angle and color in ways that did not track against the sun he occasionally glimpsed through gaps in the canopy. He set the detail aside with the rest, unwilling yet to draw a conclusion from a single day's observation, though he could feel, even in his own reluctance to conclude anything, how quickly the accumulating strangeness of the place was beginning to feel less like coincidence and more like a pattern with a cause he could not yet name.

The island's spiraling geography disoriented him more thoroughly than he expected, a slow confusion that built without any single moment he could point to as its cause. An hour into his search he found himself retracing ground he was almost certain he had already covered, the light overhead shifting in ways that made judging direction by it useless. It was, he realized with something close to genuine alarm, the first time in his long career that he had actually lost his way — a disorientation total enough that he stopped moving altogether, forcing himself to breathe through the rising unease rather than push blindly against it.

He steadied himself by turning his attention to Silens rather than the shifting ground, trusting the staff's own quiet pulse over anything his eyes or his failing sense of direction could currently offer. The pulse held steady even where the ground did not, and he followed it rather than his own increasingly unreliable instincts, one careful step at a time back toward whatever had first drawn him here.

It struck him, moving carefully forward, as an uncomfortable thing to learn this late in his service — that a scout's greatest asset could still fail him completely against ground built on principles he had never met before. He did not resent the lesson. He wondered instead, more honestly than he liked, whether he had grown too settled in the confidence his record had earned him, too willing to assume patience alone would always answer whatever the work asked of him. He filed the question away unresolved, alongside everything else Turein had already taught him.

His own pulse had climbed higher than he cared to admit during the worst of it, an unfamiliar tightness in his chest he had to breathe through deliberately. He had faced hostile terrain before, and hostile soldiers, and had met both with the same even composure that had defined his service. This was different — a threat with no shape to brace against, only his own senses failing him quietly, one step at a time. He steadied his breathing before moving again, unwilling to let the fear compound whatever the island's geometry was already doing to his sense of direction.

He found it near the island's center, after the better part of another hour spent retracing his own uncertain path, in a small clearing where the spiraling growth opened onto bare stone: a resonance unmistakably tied to Solmire, faint and old, carrying none of the sword's usual clean authority — something closer to memory than presence, as though the blade itself, or some echo of it, had passed through this exact place long before Cassiel had ever heard its name. He held the reading a long while, letting Silens confirm what his own instinct had already suspected since he first set foot on the island's shifting shore, and made himself wait a moment longer still before trusting the confirmation completely. He had been wrong about smaller things than this before. He preferred, where he could, to be wrong slowly.

Whatever this island actually was, whatever thread connected it to the sword now hanging at Miguel's side, Cassiel understood, kneeling over the resonance, that he had found something worth the trouble of returning for — and something he would need to hold carefully, the way he held any soul not yet ready to be moved.
